This replaces the two generators of the ideal by one element while retaining both degree and fiber weight.

We retain the coefficient ring and ideal from [prop:graded](#prop:graded): $$R=\mathbb C[x,y,z,u]/\bigl(xy-z(z+1)\bigr),\qquad
 s_R=y-x(x-u^3),\qquad
 I=(f,g)=(x-s_R^2-u^3,\ z-s_Rx).$$ The inclusion $R\subset G=R[\tau,I/\tau^2]$ is the actual coefficient inclusion in $R[\tau,\tau^{-1}]$. In terms of the initial generators, it is given by <a id="bun:coefficient-inclusion"></a>$$\label{bun:coefficient-inclusion}
 x=s_R^2+u^3+\tau^2(f/\tau^2),\qquad
 y=s_R+x(x-u^3),\qquad
 z=s_Rx+\tau^2(g/\tau^2).$$ This is the inclusion used in the base change below. Its elements have valuation degree zero.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### A line bundle that principalizes the center

The bundle used here is the standard quotient of $\mathrm{SL}_2$ by its diagonal torus, with the coordinate $u$ adjoined; see Blanc–van Santen <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[BlancVanSanten](#ref-BlancVanSanten), p. 8431] for this description of the affine quadric. We give its charts explicitly because the transition functions determine the fiber weights.

<a id="bun:determinant-ring"></a>
Set $$\label{bun:determinant-ring}
 \widetilde R=\mathbb C[a,d,b,c,u]/(ac-bd-1),\qquad
 x=ab,\quad y=dc,\quad z=db,$$ and let <a id="bun:v"></a>$$\label{bun:v}
 v=a^2(x-u^3)-d^2=a^3b-a^2u^3-d^2.$$ The assignments in [bun:determinant-ring](#bun:determinant-ring) define a homomorphism $R\to\widetilde R$, since $xy=(ac)(bd)=z(z+1)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-principalization"></a>
<div id="prop:principalization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9**. *The morphism $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde R\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R$ is the complement of the zero section in a line bundle, with fiber scaling specified by the weights $$\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(a)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(d)=1,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(b)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(c)=-1,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(u)=0.$$ The homomorphism $R\to\widetilde R$ is faithfully flat, and $I\widetilde R=(v)$. Moreover, base change gives a faithfully flat inclusion $G\hookrightarrow\widetilde G$, where <a id="bun:pullback-algebra"></a>$$\label{bun:pullback-algebra}
 \widetilde G:=G\otimes_R\widetilde R
   =\widetilde R[\tau,v/\tau^2]\subset\widetilde R[\tau,\tau^{-1}],\qquad
 \widetilde G\cong\widetilde R[\tau,V]/(\tau^2V-v).$$ Thus $V$ denotes the element $v/\tau^2$ in the displayed subalgebra. The ring $\widetilde G$ is a domain and has the following commuting gradings:*

<div class="center">

|                    | *$a,d$* | *$b,c$* | *$u$* | *$\tau$* | *$V$* |
|:------------------:|--------:|--------:|------:|---------:|------:|
| *Valuation degree* |   *$0$* |   *$0$* | *$0$* |   *$-1$* | *$2$* |
|   *Fiber weight*   |   *$1$* |  *$-1$* | *$0$* |    *$0$* | *$2$* |

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-principalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-principalization</summary>

*Proof.* First, $\widetilde R$ is a domain: the polynomial $ac-(bd+1)$ is primitive as a polynomial in $c$ over the unique factorization domain $\mathbb C[a,d,b,u]$, and is irreducible over its fraction field because it is linear. The element $v$ is nonzero, since $u$ is an indeterminate over the determinant ring and its coefficient in degree three in $v$ is $-a^2\ne0$.

The principal opens $D(z+1)$ and $D(z)$ cover $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R$. Over the first open, $ac=z+1$ is invertible, and the formulas <a id="bun:first-chart"></a>$$\label{bun:first-chart}
 c=\frac{z+1}{a},\qquad b=\frac{x}{a},\qquad
 d=\frac{ay}{z+1}$$ give mutually inverse identifications $$\widetilde R[(z+1)^{-1}]
   \cong R[(z+1)^{-1}][a,a^{-1}].$$ Indeed, they give $ac=z+1$, $ab=x$, $dc=y$, and $db=xy/(z+1)=z$. Over the second open, the analogous formulas are <a id="bun:second-chart"></a>$$\label{bun:second-chart}
 b=\frac{z}{d},\qquad c=\frac{y}{d},\qquad a=\frac{dx}{z},
 \qquad
 \widetilde R[z^{-1}]\cong R[z^{-1}][d,d^{-1}].$$ Here $db=z$, $dc=y$, $ab=x$, and $ac=xy/z=z+1$. On the overlap the fiber parameters satisfy $$d=\frac{y}{z+1}a,\qquad
 \frac{y}{z+1}\cdot\frac{x}{z}=1.$$ Thus their transition function is a base unit. Gluing affine lines by this same transition function gives a line bundle whose complement of the zero section is $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde R$. The indicated weights are exactly scalar multiplication on the two fiber parameters. Each Laurent polynomial chart is faithfully flat over its base chart: as a module it is free with basis the integral powers of its fiber parameter. Since the base charts cover, $R\to\widetilde R$ is faithfully flat.

We prove principalization by global identities. In $\widetilde R$ put $$h=x-u^3,\qquad C_1=c^2-b^2h,\qquad
 \alpha=a^2(1-2bd),\qquad
 \beta=d^2(3+2bd)+\alpha h.$$ Since $s_R=dc-abh$ and $ac-bd=1$, we have $$as_R-d=-bv,\qquad
 g=b(d-as_R)=b^2v.$$ Expansion also gives $$f-C_1v
  =h-(dc-abh)^2-(c^2-b^2h)(a^2h-d^2)
  =h\bigl(1-(ac-bd)^2\bigr)=0.$$ Finally, with $q=bd$ the coefficient identity is $$\begin{aligned}
 \alpha C_1+\beta b^2
 &= (ac)^2(1-2bd)+3b^2d^2+2b^3d^3\\
 &= (1+q)^2(1-2q)+3q^2+2q^3=1.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently <a id="bun:principalization-certificate"></a>$$\label{bun:principalization-certificate}
 f=C_1v,\qquad g=b^2v,\qquad v=\alpha f+\beta g,$$ which proves $I\widetilde R=(v)$.

Flatness permits us to tensor the injection $G\hookrightarrow R[\tau,\tau^{-1}]$ with $\widetilde R$. The resulting injection has image generated by $\widetilde R$, $\tau$, $f/\tau^2$, and $g/\tau^2$. By [bun:principalization-certificate](#bun:principalization-certificate), this image is precisely $\widetilde R[\tau,v/\tau^2]$. In particular it is a domain. The map $G\to G\otimes_R\widetilde R$ is faithfully flat by base change, and hence injective.

For completeness, the single displayed relation is the entire presentation. Let $W$ be an indeterminate and set $Q=\widetilde R[\tau,W]/(\tau^2W-v)$. If $\tau F=(\tau^2W-v)B$ in $\widetilde R[\tau,W]$, reduction modulo $\tau$ gives $vB(0,W)=0$. Since $\widetilde R[W]$ is a domain and $v\ne0$, we have $B=\tau B_1$; cancellation then gives $F=(\tau^2W-v)B_1$. Thus $\tau$ is a nonzerodivisor in $Q$. After inverting $\tau$, the relation eliminates $W$ and identifies $Q[\tau^{-1}]$ with $\widetilde R[\tau,\tau^{-1}]$. It follows that $Q$ injects into that Laurent ring with image $\widetilde R[\tau,v/\tau^2]$, proving [bun:pullback-algebra](#bun:pullback-algebra). The two gradings follow from $\deg(v)=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(v)=2$; the relation $\tau^2V=v$ is homogeneous for each. ◻

</details>

Write $Y=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R$ and $Z=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}G$. The geometric content of [bun:pullback-algebra](#bun:pullback-algebra) is the Cartesian square $$\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
 \mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde G\arrow[r] \arrow[d] & \mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde R\arrow[d] \\
 Z \arrow[r] & Y.
\end{tikzcd}$$ If $L_Y$ is the line bundle just constructed on $Y$, then $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde G=L^\times$, where $L$ is its pullback to $Z$ and $L^\times$ denotes the complement of its zero section.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### A normalized lift on a line bundle

The pullback is a line bundle over $Z=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}G$. An additive action on $Z$ need not descend to $Y=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}R$, so a lift obtained by first acting on $Y$ would not suffice. Instead, we prove a lifting statement for any line bundle over a smooth integral affine base. The two ingredients are invariance of the Picard group under adjoining a variable and the absence of nonconstant polynomial units over a domain.

For a variety $X$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(X)$ is the group of isomorphism classes of line bundles on $X$, with tensor product as its operation. For a line bundle $L$ on $X$, normalization means that the restriction over $\{0\}\times X$ is the identity on $L$. The Picard-group assertion is the regular-ring case of Traverso’s theorem <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Traverso](#ref-Traverso), Theorem 3.6]. Additive linearization also follows from the equivariant Picard-group results of Brion <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Brion](#ref-Brion), Proposition 2.10 and Theorem 2.14]. For the normalized isomorphism and cocycle verification we follow <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Brion](#ref-Brion), Lemma 2.9]; we include the argument to make the subsequent compatibility with valuation degree explicit.

<a id="stmt-prop-linearization"></a>
<div id="prop:linearization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10**. *Let $X$ be a smooth integral affine variety over $\mathbb C$, and let $\pi\colon\mathbb A^1\times X\to X$ be the projection. Then $$\pi^*\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(X)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
                  \mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}(\mathbb A^1\times X).$$ For every algebraic additive action $\alpha\colon\mathbb G_a\times X\to X$ and every line bundle $L$ on $X$, there is a unique isomorphism of line bundles <a id="bun:linearization-map"></a>$$\label{bun:linearization-map}
 \phi\colon\pi^*L\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^*L$$ whose restriction to $\{0\}\times X$ is $\mathrm{id}_L$. This isomorphism obeys the action cocycle. It therefore defines a unique action on $L$ that lifts $\alpha$, is linear on its fibers, and acts as the identity at the group identity. The action preserves $L^\times$ and commutes with fiber scaling. If $L^\times$ is affine, the induced derivation of its coordinate ring is locally nilpotent and preserves the fiber weights.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-linearization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-linearization</summary>

*Proof.* Put $C=\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)$ and $K=\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}C$. Both $X$ and $\mathbb A^1\times X$ are regular. Their local rings are factorial, so every Weil divisor is Cartier and their Picard groups identify with their divisor class groups <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Tags 056S, 0AG0, and 0BE9]. Let $D$ be a Weil divisor on $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}C[t]$. Its restriction to $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}K[t]$ is principal, since $K[t]$ is a principal ideal domain. Choose $r\in K(t)^*$ giving that divisor. Subtracting $\operatorname{div}(r)$ from $D$ removes every prime component whose defining prime ideal has zero contraction to $C$.

Each remaining component has a height-one prime ideal $\mathfrak P\subset C[t]$ with nonzero contraction. Choose $0\ne c\in\mathfrak P\cap C$, and choose a prime $\mathfrak q$ minimal over $(c)$ and contained in $\mathfrak P\cap C$. The principal ideal theorem <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Tag 00KV] and the fact that $C$ is a domain give $\operatorname{ht}\mathfrak q=1$. Now $\mathfrak qC[t]$ is a nonzero prime contained in the height-one prime $\mathfrak P$, so $\mathfrak P=\mathfrak qC[t]$. The corresponding divisor is the pullback of the prime divisor defined by $\mathfrak q$, with multiplicity one: a uniformizer of the discrete valuation ring $C_{\mathfrak q}$ is also a uniformizer at the generic point of that pulled-back divisor. Thus every divisor class on $\mathbb A^1\times X$ is pulled back from $X$, proving surjectivity of $\pi^*$. Restriction to $i_0\colon X\to\mathbb A^1\times X$, $x\mapsto(0,x)$, is a left inverse to $\pi^*$ on Picard groups, proving injectivity as well.

Since $\alpha\circ i_0=\pi\circ i_0=\mathrm{id}_X$, the line bundles $\alpha^*L$ and $\pi^*L$ have the same restriction to the identity section. The Picard isomorphism just proved implies that they are isomorphic. Take an isomorphism $\phi_1$ between them, with the orientation in [bun:linearization-map](#bun:linearization-map). Its restriction $i_0^*\phi_1$ is an automorphism of $L$. Composing on the source with $\pi^*((i_0^*\phi_1)^{-1})$ produces an isomorphism $\phi$ whose restriction is the identity.

An automorphism of any line bundle on a scheme is multiplication by a global unit: its endomorphism sheaf is canonically the structure sheaf. Because $C$ is a domain, <a id="bun:parameter-units"></a>$$\label{bun:parameter-units}
 C[t]^*=C^*,\qquad C[s,t]^*=C^*.$$ Indeed, total degree of a product of nonzero polynomials over a domain is the sum of their total degrees, so a polynomial unit has degree zero. The ratio of two normalized isomorphisms in [bun:linearization-map](#bun:linearization-map) is a unit of $C[t]$ whose value at $t=0$ is one. By [bun:parameter-units](#bun:parameter-units), that ratio is one. This proves uniqueness, without a restriction on the units of $C$.

To check the cocycle, denote the fiber map by $\phi_{t,x}\colon L_x\to L_{\alpha(t,x)}$. Over $\mathbb A^2\times X$, the two compositions to compare are $$\phi_{t,\alpha(s,x)}\circ\phi_{s,x},\quad
 \phi_{s+t,x}
 \colon L_x\longrightarrow L_{\alpha(s+t,x)}.$$ Their sources and targets agree by the action law for $\alpha$. Thus $$\phi_{s+t,x}^{-1}\circ
 \phi_{t,\alpha(s,x)}\circ\phi_{s,x}$$ is an automorphism of the pullback of $L$ from $X$, hence a unit of $C[s,t]$. At $s=0$ it is the identity. By [bun:parameter-units](#bun:parameter-units), it is the identity everywhere. This proves the cocycle. The family of fiber maps consequently defines an algebraic additive action on the total space of $L$. Linearity preserves zero and nonzero vectors and commutes with scalar multiplication, giving the asserted action on $L^\times$.

Finally, suppose $L^\times=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}T$ is affine. Write its action on functions as $$\rho(r)=\sum_{n\ge0}\delta_n(r)t^n\in T[t],\qquad r\in T,$$ where the sum is finite for each $r$. The identity and action laws give $\delta_0=\mathrm{id}$ and $$\delta_i\delta_j=\binom{i+j}{i}\delta_{i+j}.$$ The coefficient of $t$ in multiplicativity of $\rho$ says that $\delta_1$ is a derivation. Since the characteristic is zero, the displayed composition identity gives $\delta_n=\delta_1^n/n!$. Finiteness of $\rho(r)$ now says that $\delta_1$ is locally nilpotent. Commutation with fiber scaling says precisely that this derivation preserves the fiber weights. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Compatibility with valuation degree

The preceding proposition gives a lift preserving fiber weights. We still need its valuation degree to agree with that of the original derivation. The degree action has its own explicit bundle lift; uniqueness of the normalized additive lift makes the two compatible.

<a id="stmt-prop-homogeneous-lift"></a>
<div id="prop:homogeneous-lift" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11**. *Let $D_0$ be a homogeneous locally nilpotent derivation of $G$ of valuation degree shift $j$. There is a locally nilpotent derivation $\widetilde D$ of $\widetilde G$ restricting to $D_0$ on $G$, with fiber weight zero and valuation degree shift $j$. If $D_0\ne0$, then $\widetilde D\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-homogeneous-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-homogeneous-lift</summary>

*Proof.* By [prop:graded](#prop:graded), $Z=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}G$ is smooth and integral. Exponentiating $D_0$ gives the algebraic action on functions $$\exp(tD_0)(r)=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{D_0^n(r)}{n!}t^n.$$ Apply [prop:linearization](#prop:linearization) to this action and the line bundle $L$ on $Z$ whose complement is $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\widetilde G$. The resulting locally nilpotent derivation $\widetilde D$ restricts to $D_0$ because the action covers the given action on $Z$, and has fiber weight zero because the lift commutes with fiber scaling. Since $G\hookrightarrow\widetilde G$, a nonzero $D_0$ gives a nonzero $\widetilde D$.

It remains to establish its valuation degree. For $\lambda\in\mathbb C^*$, let $\sigma_\lambda$ be the automorphism of $G$ multiplying a homogeneous element of degree $h$ by $\lambda^h$. It fixes the coefficient subring $R$ pointwise. Therefore it has the pullback lift $\widetilde\sigma_\lambda=\sigma_\lambda\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\widetilde R}$ on $\widetilde G$. Explicitly, <a id="bun:degree-scaling"></a>$$\label{bun:degree-scaling}
 \widetilde\sigma_\lambda(r)=r\quad(r\in\widetilde R),\qquad
 \widetilde\sigma_\lambda(\tau)=\lambda^{-1}\tau,\qquad
 \widetilde\sigma_\lambda(V)=\lambda^2V.$$ Geometrically this lift acts on $L=Z\times_Y L_Y$ by the degree action on $Z$ and the identity on $L_Y$. In particular it extends to a linear bundle automorphism, and its action on the complement agrees with [bun:degree-scaling](#bun:degree-scaling).

On the coordinate ring of the base, homogeneity of $D_0$ gives <a id="bun:base-conjugacy"></a>$$\label{bun:base-conjugacy}
 \sigma_\lambda D_0\sigma_\lambda^{-1}=\lambda^jD_0.$$ Conjugate the lifted additive action by $\widetilde\sigma_\lambda$, using the expression on coordinate rings $$\widetilde\sigma_\lambda\,\exp(t\widetilde D)\,
 \widetilde\sigma_\lambda^{-1}.$$ This is a normalized linear lift of the base action $\exp(\lambda^jtD_0)$, by [bun:base-conjugacy](#bun:base-conjugacy). The action $\exp(\lambda^jt\widetilde D)$ is another normalized linear lift of that same base action. Uniqueness in [prop:linearization](#prop:linearization) therefore gives equality of these two families. Taking their coefficients of $t$ yields $$\widetilde\sigma_\lambda\widetilde D
 \widetilde\sigma_\lambda^{-1}=\lambda^j\widetilde D
 \qquad(\lambda\in\mathbb C^*).$$ For a homogeneous $r$ of degree $h$, this says $\widetilde\sigma_\lambda(\widetilde Dr)=\lambda^{h+j}\widetilde Dr$ for every $\lambda$. The distinct integral characters of $\mathbb G_m$ are linearly independent, so $\widetilde Dr$ is homogeneous of degree $h+j$ whenever it is nonzero. This proves the required degree shift. ◻

</details>

The additive lift in [prop:homogeneous-lift](#prop:homogeneous-lift) is constructed on the line bundle over $Z=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}G$. It does not require $D_0(R)\subset R$, and it does not assert $\widetilde D(\widetilde R)\subset\widetilde R$. The invariance of coefficient rings used in the proof belongs to the separate degree action: $\sigma_\lambda$ fixes $R$, and $\widetilde\sigma_\lambda$ fixes $\widetilde R$.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### Extracting a derivation on the determinant ring

We now pass from a homogeneous additive action on the degeneration to a locally nilpotent derivation of $$\widetilde R=\mathbb C[a,d,b,c,u]/(ac-bd-1)$$ with a prescribed sign for its fiber weight. Throughout this section, *degree* means the valuation degree, so that $$\deg \widetilde R=0,\qquad \deg\tau=-1,\qquad \deg V=2.$$ The separate fiber weights are $$\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(a)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(d)=1,\quad \mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(b)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(c)=-1,\quad
  \mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(u)=\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(\tau)=0,\quad \mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(V)=2.$$ By [prop:principalization](#prop:principalization), we work in the domain <a id="red:ring"></a>$$\label{red:ring}
  \widetilde G=\widetilde R[\tau,V]\subset\widetilde R[\tau,\tau^{-1}],
  \qquad \tau^2V=v=a^3b-a^2u^3-d^2.$$ In particular, $\tau$, $V$, and $v$ are nonzero. The notation $\widetilde R[\tau,V]$ here denotes the indicated subalgebra, with the displayed relation.

The positive-degree invariant supplied by polynomiality will be divisible by $V$. The elementary order of a locally nilpotent derivation makes its kernel factorially closed, so this divisibility will force $V$ itself to be invariant. We recall the argument, including the order estimates needed when $\tau$ moves; compare Finston–Maubach <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[FinstonMaubach](#ref-FinstonMaubach), Lemma 1.1].

<a id="stmt-lem-lnd-order"></a>
<div id="lem:lnd-order" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Order of a locally nilpotent derivation). *Let $T$ be a domain over $\mathbb C$ and let $\delta$ be a locally nilpotent derivation of $T$. For $0\ne h\in T$, define $$\nu_\delta(h)=\max\{n\ge0:\delta^n(h)\ne0\}.$$ Then for nonzero $f,g\in T$, <a id="red:order-product"></a>$$\label{red:order-product}
  \nu_\delta(fg)=\nu_\delta(f)+\nu_\delta(g).$$ If $\delta(h)\ne0$, then $\nu_\delta(\delta(h))=\nu_\delta(h)-1$. Consequently, $\ker\delta$ is factorially closed in $T$: if $0\ne fg\in\ker\delta$, then $f,g\in\ker\delta$. Moreover, $\delta(h)\ne0$ implies $\delta(h)\notin hT$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lnd-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lnd-order</summary>

*Proof.* Local nilpotence makes the defining maximum finite. Set $r=\nu_\delta(f)$ and $s=\nu_\delta(g)$. The iterated Leibniz rule gives $$\delta^n(fg)=\sum_{i=0}^n\binom ni\delta^i(f)\delta^{n-i}(g).$$ For $n>r+s$ every term is zero. For $n=r+s$ only the term with $i=r$ can be nonzero, and it equals $$\binom{r+s}{r}\delta^r(f)\delta^s(g)\ne0.$$ Here we use both characteristic zero and the domain property. This proves [red:order-product](#red:order-product). The assertion about $\delta(h)$ follows directly from the definition. Since a nonzero element has order zero exactly when it is invariant, product additivity proves factorial closure. Finally, if $0\ne\delta(h)=hq$, then $q\ne0$ and $$\nu_\delta(h)-1
    =\nu_\delta(\delta(h))
    =\nu_\delta(h)+\nu_\delta(q)
    \ge\nu_\delta(h),$$ which is impossible. ◻

</details>

We will also use the exact homogeneous pieces of [red:ring](#red:ring). Writing $\widetilde G_e$ for its degree-$e$ component, they are <a id="red:pieces"></a>$$\label{red:pieces}
  \widetilde G_e=
  \begin{cases}
    \tau^{-e}\widetilde R, & e<0,\\
    V^\ell\widetilde R, & e=2\ell,\quad \ell\ge0,\\
    \tau V^{\ell+1}\widetilde R, & e=2\ell+1,\quad \ell\ge0.
  \end{cases}$$ Indeed, a degree-$e$ element is an $\widetilde R$-linear combination of monomials $\tau^iV^h$ with $i,h\ge0$ and $2h-i=e$. Using $\tau^2V=v$, these monomials become, respectively, $$\tau^{-e}v^h,\qquad
  V^\ell v^{h-\ell},\qquad
  \tau V^{\ell+1}v^{h-\ell-1}.$$ The exponents of $v$ in the relevant cases are nonnegative. Conversely, the multipliers displayed in [red:pieces](#red:pieces) belong to $\widetilde G$ and have the required degrees. This proves the equalities, including that their coefficients lie in $\widetilde R$. Since the multipliers are nonzero and $\widetilde G$ is a domain, each such coefficient is unique.

<a id="stmt-prop-extracted-lnd"></a>
<div id="prop:extracted-lnd" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *If $A\cong\mathbb C^{[4]}$, there are a nonzero locally nilpotent derivation $E$ of $\widetilde R$ and an integer $q\le0$ such that $E$ is homogeneous of fiber weight shift $q$ and $$E^2(v)=0.$$ Here homogeneity means that $E(r)$ has fiber weight $\mathop{\mathrm{wt}}(r)+q$ whenever $r$ is fiber homogeneous and $E(r)\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-extracted-lnd"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-extracted-lnd</summary>

*Proof.* By [prop:positive-invariant](#prop:positive-invariant), polynomiality of $A$ supplies a nonzero homogeneous LND $D_0$ of $G$ and a nonzero homogeneous $h_0\in\ker D_0$ of positive degree. Apply [prop:homogeneous-lift](#prop:homogeneous-lift) to obtain an LND $\widetilde D$ of $\widetilde G$ lifting $D_0$, homogeneous of some degree shift $j\in\mathbb Z$ and of fiber weight shift zero. The injection $G\hookrightarrow\widetilde G$ in [prop:principalization](#prop:principalization) ensures both that $\widetilde D\ne0$ and that the image $h$ of $h_0$ is nonzero. This image is a homogeneous invariant of the same positive degree. By [red:pieces](#red:pieces), $h$ is divisible by $V$ in $\widetilde G$. Factorial closure in [lem:lnd-order](#lem:lnd-order) therefore gives <a id="red:V-invariant"></a>$$\label{red:V-invariant}
  \widetilde D(V)=0.$$ We distinguish whether $\tau$ is also invariant. If it is, the derivation restricts after removing a power of $\tau$. If it is not, the homogeneous pieces force a positive factor of $V$ into its values on $\widetilde R$; removing this factor produces a derivation of negative fiber weight. The LND order proves local nilpotence in this second case without assuming that $\widetilde D$ preserves $\widetilde R$.

**Case 1: $\widetilde D(\tau)=0$.** In this case $\widetilde D$ extends to a locally nilpotent derivation of $$\widetilde G[\tau^{-1}]=\widetilde R[\tau,\tau^{-1}].$$ Indeed, for $f\in\widetilde G$ and $m\ge0$, all iterates on $f\tau^{-m}$ equal $\tau^{-m}\widetilde D^n(f)$. The degree-$j$ component of this Laurent polynomial ring is exactly $\tau^{-j}\widetilde R$. Since every $r\in\widetilde R$ has degree zero, there is thus a unique $E(r)\in\widetilde R$ satisfying <a id="red:fixed-tau"></a>$$\label{red:fixed-tau}
  \widetilde D(r)=\tau^{-j}E(r).$$ Linearity and the Leibniz rule for $\widetilde D$, followed by cancellation of $\tau^{-j}$, show that $E:\widetilde R\to\widetilde R$ is a $\mathbb C$-derivation. It has fiber weight shift zero because $\tau$ has fiber weight zero and $\widetilde D$ preserves fiber weight. Since $\widetilde D(\tau)=0$, induction in [red:fixed-tau](#red:fixed-tau) gives $$\widetilde D^n(r)=\tau^{-nj}E^n(r)\qquad(n\ge0).$$ Local nilpotence of $\widetilde D$ therefore proves local nilpotence of $E$. By [red:V-invariant](#red:V-invariant), $$0=\widetilde D(\tau^2V)=\widetilde D(v)=\tau^{-j}E(v),$$ so $E(v)=0$. Finally, $E=0$ would make $\widetilde D$ vanish on $\widetilde R$, $\tau$, and $V$, which generate $\widetilde G$; hence $E\ne0$. This proves the desired conclusion with $q=0$.

**Case 2: $\widetilde D(\tau)\ne0$.** Write $\nu=\nu_{\widetilde D}$ on the nonzero elements of $\widetilde G$, and put $m=\nu(\tau)\ge1$. By [lem:lnd-order](#lem:lnd-order), $\widetilde D(\tau)\notin\tau\widetilde G$. On the other hand, this element is homogeneous of degree $j-1$. [red:pieces](#red:pieces) shows that every component of negative degree, and every component of positive odd degree, is contained in $\tau\widetilde G$. We must therefore have <a id="red:odd-shift"></a>$$\label{red:odd-shift}
  j-1=2\ell\quad\text{for some }\ell\ge0,
  \qquad j=2\ell+1.$$ For $r\in\widetilde R$, the value $\widetilde D(r)$ belongs to the degree-$j$ piece. By [red:pieces](#red:pieces) there is a unique element $E(r)\in\widetilde R$ such that <a id="red:moving-tau"></a>$$\label{red:moving-tau}
  \widetilde D(r)=\tau V^{\ell+1}E(r).$$ Thus the values of $E$ lie in $\widetilde R$ before any iteration or nilpotence argument is used. Cancellation of the common nonzero multiplier in the linearity and Leibniz identities again proves that $E$ is a $\mathbb C$-derivation of $\widetilde R$. Since that multiplier has fiber weight $2(\ell+1)$, the fiber weight shift of $E$ is <a id="red:negative-weight"></a>$$\label{red:negative-weight}
  q=-2(\ell+1)<0.$$

We prove local nilpotence inside $\widetilde G$ using its LND order. Whenever $E(r)\ne0$, [red:moving-tau](#red:moving-tau), [red:V-invariant](#red:V-invariant), and [lem:lnd-order](#lem:lnd-order) imply <a id="red:order-decrease"></a>$$\label{red:order-decrease}
  \nu(E(r))=\nu(r)-1-m.$$ In fact, the order of the left side of [red:moving-tau](#red:moving-tau) is $\nu(r)-1$, whereas that of its right side is $m+(\ell+1)\nu(V)+\nu(E(r))=m+\nu(E(r))$. Every iterate $E^n(r)$ still lies in $\widetilde R$. Consequently, as long as it is nonzero, successive applications of [red:order-decrease](#red:order-decrease) give $$\nu(E^n(r))=\nu(r)-n(m+1).$$ This nonnegative integer cannot decrease indefinitely, proving that $E$ is locally nilpotent.

Differentiating the relation in [red:ring](#red:ring) gives <a id="red:moving-v"></a>$$\label{red:moving-v}
  \tau V^{\ell+1}E(v)=\widetilde D(v)=2\tau V\widetilde D(\tau)\ne0.$$ The last inequality uses characteristic zero and the domain property. Thus $E(v)\ne0$, and in particular $E\ne0$. Product additivity and [red:V-invariant](#red:V-invariant) give $\nu(v)=\nu(\tau^2V)=2m$; hence [red:order-decrease](#red:order-decrease) yields $$\nu(E(v))=m-1.$$ If $E^2(v)$ were nonzero, a second application would yield $$\nu(E^2(v))=(m-1)-1-m=-2,$$ an impossibility. Therefore $E^2(v)=0$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-red-sign-essential"></a>
<div id="red:sign-essential" class="remark">

*Remark 14*. The restriction to nonpositive fiber weight is essential to the obstruction proved in [sec:rigidity](#sec:rigidity). Indeed, the derivation $$E_+(b)=a,\qquad E_+(c)=d,\qquad
  E_+(a)=E_+(d)=E_+(u)=0$$ annihilates $ac-bd-1$ and so defines a derivation of $\widetilde R$. It is locally nilpotent, since on the polynomial ring it lowers total degree in $b,c$, and it has fiber weight shift $+2$. Nevertheless, $E_+(v)=a^4$ and $E_+^2(v)=0$.

</div>

<a id="sec:rigidity"></a>
### The rigidity obstruction

We now exclude the derivation supplied by [prop:extracted-lnd](#prop:extracted-lnd). Recall that $$\widetilde R=\mathbb C[a,d,b,c,u]/(ac-bd-1),\qquad
 v=a^3b-a^2u^3-d^2,$$ and that the fiber weights of $(a,d,b,c,u)$ are $(1,1,-1,-1,0)$. A derivation has fiber shift $e$ if it sends each fiber-weight-$i$ component into the fiber-weight-$(i+e)$ component.

The proof first passes to the highest part for a third, auxiliary grading. It then studies the polynomial orbit of $N=d^2+a^2u^3$. The polynomial $abc$ inequality forces this highest derivation to fix $u$, and a nonsquare in the original fraction field forces it to fix $a,d$ as well. The determinant relation leaves only derivations whose fiber weight is positive.

The use of polynomial $abc$ to constrain locally nilpotent derivations has precedents in Finston–Maubach <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[FinstonMaubach](#ref-FinstonMaubach), Section 2]. We include the Wronskian proof of the Mason–Stothers inequality <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Mason](#ref-Mason), [Stothers](#ref-Stothers)], followed by the elementary one-variable fact needed at the end of the argument.

<a id="stmt-rig-abc"></a>
<div id="rig:abc" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Mason–Stothers polynomial $abc$). *Let $F$ be a field of characteristic zero, and let $X,Y,Z\in F[q]$ be nonzero, pairwise relatively prime polynomials satisfying $X+Y=Z$. If they are not all constant, then $$\max\{\deg X,\deg Y,\deg Z\}
 \le r(XYZ)-1,$$ where $r(XYZ)$ is the number of distinct roots of $XYZ$ in an algebraic closure of $F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-abc"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-abc</summary>

*Proof.* Primes in this proof denote differentiation with respect to $q$. Consider the Wronskian $$W=X'Y-XY'.$$ It is nonzero. Indeed, if $W=0$, relative primality implies that $X$ divides $X'$. In characteristic zero this is impossible for nonconstant $X$, so $X$ is a nonzero constant. Then $Y'=0$, hence $Y$ and $Z$ are constant as well, contrary to the hypothesis.

The same Wronskian, up to sign, is obtained from either of the other two pairs, because $Z=X+Y$. Work over an algebraic closure only to count roots. A root of multiplicity $h$ of one of the three polynomials is not a root of either of the others. Computing $W$ from a pair containing that polynomial shows that $W$ vanishes at the root to order at least $h-1$. These roots are distinct across the three polynomials, so, on writing $T=\deg X+\deg Y+\deg Z$, we obtain $$\deg W\ge T-r(XYZ).$$ On the other hand, compute $W$ from the two polynomials other than one of maximum degree $M$. Differentiation lowers the degree of every nonconstant polynomial by one, and therefore $$\deg W\le T-M-1.$$ Together these inequalities give $M\le r(XYZ)-1$.

The nonconstant hypothesis is necessary: for three nonzero constants the right side would be $-1$. The nonzero hypothesis also causes no missing nonconstant case: if one member of a pairwise relatively prime triple $X+Y=Z$ is zero, the other two must be nonzero constants. The all-zero triple is not pairwise relatively prime. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rig-one-variable"></a>
<div id="rig:one-variable" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Derivations in one variable). *Let $F$ be a field of characteristic zero. If $\delta$ is a locally nilpotent $F$-derivation of $F[t]$, then $\delta(t)\in F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rig-one-variable"><summary>Proof of stmt-rig-one-variable</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\delta=P(t)\,d/dt$. There is nothing to prove if $P=0$. Otherwise let $m=\deg P$ and let $\gamma\ne0$ be its leading coefficient. If $m\ge1$, induction shows that, for every $n\ge1$, the polynomial $\delta^n(t)$ has degree $1+n(m-1)$ and leading coefficient $$\gamma^n\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\bigl(1+j(m-1)\bigr).$$ Every factor is nonzero in characteristic zero, including when $m=1$. Thus no iterate vanishes, contradicting local nilpotence. Consequently $m=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-rigidity"></a>
<div id="prop:rigidity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17**. *There is no nonzero locally nilpotent $\mathbb C$-derivation $E$ of $\widetilde R$ with a fixed fiber shift $e\le0$ and $E^2(v)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rigidity"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rigidity</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose such an $E$ exists. We introduce an *auxiliary grading*, distinct from both the valuation degree and the fiber weight: <a id="rig:auxiliary-grading"></a>$$\label{rig:auxiliary-grading}
 \begin{array}{c|rrrrr}
  &a&d&b&c&u\\ \hline
  \text{valuation degree}&0&0&0&0&0\\
  \text{fiber weight}&1&1&-1&-1&0\\
  \text{auxiliary degree}&0&3&-3&0&2.
 \end{array}$$ The relation $ac-bd-1$ is homogeneous of auxiliary degree zero, so this is an actual $\mathbb Z$-grading on $\widetilde R$. Its components need not vanish in negative degrees. The generators are homogeneous for both this grading and the fiber grading, and the relation is homogeneous for both; thus the two gradings commute.

*The highest auxiliary component.* For an auxiliary-homogeneous $r\in\widetilde R$ of degree $i$, define $E_j(r)$ to be the degree-$(i+j)$ component of $E(r)$, and extend linearly to $\widetilde R$. Projecting the product rule to each degree shows that every $E_j$ is a derivation. Only finitely many of these derivations are nonzero: their values on the five generators are obtained from the finitely many homogeneous components of their images under $E$, and a derivation vanishing on all five generators is zero. We therefore have a finite decomposition $$E=\sum_j E_j.$$ Let $\lambda$ be its greatest nonzero shift and set $E'=E_\lambda$. Then $E'\ne0$. For every auxiliary-homogeneous $r$ of degree $i$ and every $n\ge1$, the component of $E^n(r)$ of degree $i+n\lambda$ equals $(E')^n(r)$: in the expansion of the iterate, each chosen shift is at most $\lambda$, and their sum can equal $n\lambda$ only when every chosen shift equals $\lambda$. Local nilpotence of $E$ therefore implies local nilpotence of $E'$ on every homogeneous $r$, and hence on every element, which is a finite sum of such elements. This reasoning uses finite shift sets and finite sums, so the negative degrees in [rig:auxiliary-grading](#rig:auxiliary-grading) cause no difficulty. Projection to auxiliary degree preserves fiber weight, and consequently $E'$ still has fiber shift $e$.

Set $$N=d^2+a^2u^3.$$ Both its summands have auxiliary degree $6$, whereas $a^3b$ has auxiliary degree $-3$. In $E^2(v)=E^2(a^3b-N)$, the first term has auxiliary degrees at most $-3+2\lambda$. The degree $6+2\lambda$ component of the second term is $(E')^2(N)$. Taking that component of $E^2(v)=0$ gives <a id="rig:linear-orbit"></a>$$\label{rig:linear-orbit}
 (E')^2(N)=0.$$

*The orbit polynomials fix $a,d,u$.* The localization obtained by solving for $c$ is <a id="rig:a-chart"></a>$$\label{rig:a-chart}
 \widetilde R[a^{-1}]=\mathbb C[a,a^{-1},d,u][b],
 \qquad c=\frac{1+bd}{a}.$$ In particular, $a,d,b,u$ are algebraically independent, $N\ne0$, and the fraction field is <a id="rig:original-field"></a>$$\label{rig:original-field}
 K=\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(\widetilde R)=\mathbb C(a,d,b)(u).$$ Since $E'$ is locally nilpotent, exponentiating it gives polynomials $$a(q)=\exp(qE')(a),\quad
 d(q)=\exp(qE')(d),\quad
 u(q)=\exp(qE')(u)
 \quad\text{in }\widetilde R[q]\subset K[q].$$ Each is nonzero, since its value at $q=0$ is the corresponding nonzero element of $\widetilde R$. The exponential is multiplicative by the iterated product rule. Hence [rig:linear-orbit](#rig:linear-orbit) implies <a id="rig:orbit-sum"></a>$$\label{rig:orbit-sum}
 d(q)^2+a(q)^2u(q)^3
 =N(q):=\exp(qE')(N)=N+qE'(N),
 \qquad \deg_q N(q)\le1.$$ Here $N(q)\ne0$ because $N(0)=N\ne0$. Thus neither summand nor their sum is the zero polynomial.

The three polynomials in [rig:orbit-sum](#rig:orbit-sum) are pairwise relatively prime in $K[q]$. To see this, a common root of the two summands over $\overline K$ would have multiplicity at least two in each summand. For the second summand this follows whether the root belongs to $a(q)$, to $u(q)$, or to both. Their sum would then have a root of multiplicity at least two, which a nonzero polynomial of degree at most one cannot have. Thus the summands are relatively prime, and the identity $X+Y=Z$ implies relative primality of the other two pairs. No relative primality between $a(q)$ and $u(q)$ is needed.

Write $$D_a=\deg_q a(q),\qquad D_d=\deg_q d(q),\qquad
 D_u=\deg_q u(q).$$ Suppose at least one summand in [rig:orbit-sum](#rig:orbit-sum) is nonconstant. A nonconstant summand has degree at least two. Since the sum has degree at most one, both summands must then have the same degree $$M=2D_d=2D_a+3D_u\ge2.$$ The number of distinct roots of their product with $N(q)$ is at most $D_d+D_a+D_u+\deg_q N(q)$. Applying [rig:abc](#rig:abc) therefore yields <a id="rig:degree-bound"></a>$$\label{rig:degree-bound}
 2D_d=2D_a+3D_u
 \le D_d+D_a+D_u+\deg_q N(q)-1
 \le D_d+D_a+D_u.$$ This also covers constant $N(q)$; in that case the first bound is stronger. The last inequality gives $D_d\le D_a+D_u$. Combining it with $2D_d=2D_a+3D_u$ gives $3D_u\le2D_u$, so $D_u=0$. Consequently $u(q)=u$ and $D_d=D_a>0$.

Let $\alpha,\delta\in K^*$ be the leading coefficients of $a(q)$ and $d(q)$, respectively. Cancellation of the degree-$M$ terms in [rig:orbit-sum](#rig:orbit-sum) requires $$\delta^2+\alpha^2u^3=0,
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 \left(\frac{\delta}{\alpha}\right)^2=-u^3.$$ This equality would hold in the original field $K$ of [rig:original-field](#rig:original-field), not merely in $\overline K$. It is impossible: put $K_0=\mathbb C(a,d,b)$, so that $K=K_0(u)$. For a nonzero rational function of $u$, the multiplicity of $u$ in its numerator minus its multiplicity in its denominator defines an integer-valued order. A square has even order, while $-u^3$ has order three. Passing to $\overline K$ above was used only for counting roots and has not changed the field of the leading coefficients.

We conclude that both summands in [rig:orbit-sum](#rig:orbit-sum) are constant. Their nonvanishing gives $2D_d=0$ and $2D_a+3D_u=0$. All three degrees are nonnegative, so $D_a=D_d=D_u=0$. Taking coefficients of $q$ in their exponentials now gives <a id="rig:fixed-coefficients"></a>$$\label{rig:fixed-coefficients}
 E'(a)=E'(d)=E'(u)=0.$$

*The remaining coefficient has an impossible fiber weight.* Differentiating $ac-bd=1$ and using [rig:fixed-coefficients](#rig:fixed-coefficients) gives $aE'(c)=dE'(b)$. Define $$h=cE'(b)-bE'(c)\in\widetilde R.$$ Using both determinant identities, without dividing by either $a$ or $d$, gives <a id="rig:h-formulas"></a>$$\label{rig:h-formulas}
 a h=(ac-bd)E'(b)=E'(b),\qquad
 d h=(ac-bd)E'(c)=E'(c).$$ Let $C_0=\mathbb C[a,d,u]$ and $Q=\mathop{\mathrm{Frac}}(C_0)=\mathbb C(a,d,u)$. By [rig:fixed-coefficients](#rig:fixed-coefficients), every nonzero element of $C_0$ is invariant. Thus $E'$ extends to a locally nilpotent $Q$-derivation of $$(C_0\setminus\{0\})^{-1}\widetilde R=Q[b],$$ where the equality follows by solving for $c$ as in [rig:a-chart](#rig:a-chart). Local nilpotence is preserved because $(E')^n(r/s)=(E')^n(r)/s$ for $s\in C_0\setminus\{0\}$. By [rig:one-variable](#rig:one-variable), $E'(b)\in Q$; hence [rig:h-formulas](#rig:h-formulas) implies $h\in Q$.

We spell out the coefficient intersection that improves this to $h\in C_0$. Besides [rig:a-chart](#rig:a-chart), solving for $b$ gives $$\widetilde R[d^{-1}]=\mathbb C[a,d,d^{-1},u][c],
 \qquad b=\frac{ac-1}{d}.$$ Since $b$ and $c$ are each transcendental over $Q$, comparison of polynomial coefficients in these two presentations gives $$\widetilde R\cap Q
 \subset C_0[a^{-1}]\cap C_0[d^{-1}]
 =C_0.$$ The last intersection is taken in $Q$: in the unique factorization domain $C_0$, the reduced denominator of an element belonging to both rings must divide a power of $a$ and a power of $d$. As $a$ and $d$ are relatively prime, that denominator is a unit. Conversely $C_0\subset\widetilde R\cap Q$, so the intersection is exactly $C_0$. In particular $h\in C_0$.

The derivation $E'$ is nonzero and fixes $a,d,u$; therefore [rig:h-formulas](#rig:h-formulas) implies $h\ne0$. Since $E'$ has fiber shift $e$, the definition of $h$ shows that it is homogeneous of fiber weight $e-2<0$. But every monomial in $C_0=\mathbb C[a,d,u]$ has nonnegative fiber weight, because the weights of $a,d,u$ are $1,1,0$. A nonzero element of $C_0$ cannot have negative fiber weight. This contradiction proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rigidity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rigidity</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* The explicitly defined algebra $A=P/(H)$ is a finitely generated unital $\mathbb C$-algebra. By [con:domain](#con:domain) it is integral, with fraction field $\mathbb C(p,x,y,z)$, and hence has dimension four. Moreover, [prop:stabilization](#prop:stabilization) gives a $\mathbb C$-algebra isomorphism $$A[w]\cong\mathbb C^{[5]}.$$ Suppose that $A\cong\mathbb C^{[4]}$. By [prop:graded](#prop:graded), [prop:positive-invariant](#prop:positive-invariant), its associated graded ring $G$ admits a nonzero homogeneous locally nilpotent derivation $D_0$ with a nonzero homogeneous invariant of positive valuation degree. The principal bundle and its coordinate ring $\widetilde G$ are given by [prop:principalization](#prop:principalization). By [prop:homogeneous-lift](#prop:homogeneous-lift), $D_0$ lifts to a locally nilpotent derivation $\widetilde D$ on $\widetilde G$ with the same valuation degree shift and fiber shift zero. The reduction of this lift and invariant in [prop:extracted-lnd](#prop:extracted-lnd) produces a nonzero locally nilpotent derivation $E$ of $\widetilde R$ with a fixed nonpositive fiber shift and $E^2(v)=0$. This contradicts [prop:rigidity](#prop:rigidity). Thus $A\not\cong\mathbb C^{[4]}$, while its cylinder is $\mathbb C^{[5]}$. Taking $n=4$ proves the claimed counterexample over $\mathbb C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:affine-fibrations"></a>
### Nontrivial affine-space fibrations

The residual-coordinate property of $H$ determines the fibers of two natural morphisms. We first use it to prove that the morphisms are smooth, then use the nonpolynomiality of $A$ to rule out Zariski local triviality.

<a id="stmt-cor-affine-fibrations"></a>
<div id="cor:affine-fibrations" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18** (Nontrivial smooth $\mathbb A^3$-fibrations). *Let $P,H,A$ be as in <a href="#eq:example" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:example">[eq:example]</a>, and put $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$. Write $\mathbb A^1_p=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb C[p]$ and $\mathbb A^2_{p,q}=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb C[p,q]$, where $q$ is independent of $p$. Consider the morphisms $$\pi:X\longrightarrow\mathbb A^1_p,
 \qquad
 \rho=(p,H):\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}P=\mathbb A^5\longrightarrow\mathbb A^2_{p,q},$$ induced respectively by $\mathbb C[p]\to A$ and by $\mathbb C[p,q]\to P$ with $q\mapsto H$. Both are smooth surjective morphisms of relative dimension three. For either morphism and every point $y$ of its base, the fiber over $y$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb A^3_{\kappa(y)}$; in particular, every geometric fiber is affine three-space. Neither morphism is a Zariski-locally trivial $\mathbb A^3$-bundle. Thus the Dolgachev–Weisfeiler affine-fibration conjecture fails in relative dimension three over each of the smooth complex affine bases $\mathbb A^1$ and $\mathbb A^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-affine-fibrations"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-affine-fibrations</summary>

<a id="fib:residual-coordinate"></a>
*Proof.* Set $\Lambda=\mathbb C[p]$ and write $\mathbf z=(s,u,F,J)$. For every prime $\mathfrak p$ of $\Lambda$, let $H_{\mathfrak p}$ denote the image of $H$ in $\kappa(\mathfrak p)[\mathbf z]$. The coordinate changes in [con:localized-coordinates](#con:localized-coordinates), [con:localized-h](#con:localized-h) show that $H$ is a coordinate over $\mathbb C[p,p^{-1}]$. At $\mathfrak p=(p)$, the determinant-one change in [con:linear-coordinates](#con:linear-coordinates), [con:linear-inverse](#con:linear-inverse) gives $H_{\mathfrak p}=L\bmod p$. Every other prime of $\Lambda$ avoids $p$. It follows that for every $\mathfrak p$ there are three polynomials $G_1,G_2,G_3$ over $\kappa(\mathfrak p)$ such that $$\label{fib:residual-coordinate}
 \kappa(\mathfrak p)[\mathbf z]
 =\kappa(\mathfrak p)[H_{\mathfrak p},G_1,G_2,G_3]$$ is a polynomial coordinate system.

Let $y$ be any point of $\mathbb A^2_{p,q}$, set $\mathfrak p=\ker(\Lambda\to\kappa(y))$, and let $b$ be the image of $q$ in $\kappa(y)$. The field $\kappa(y)$ is an extension of $\kappa(\mathfrak p)$. Tensoring <a href="#fib:residual-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fib:residual-coordinate">[fib:residual-coordinate]</a> with $\kappa(y)$ and then setting the coordinate $H_{\mathfrak p}$ equal to $b$ gives $$\rho_y
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\bigl(\kappa(y)[\mathbf z]/(H_{\mathfrak p}-b)\bigr)
 \simeq\mathbb A^3_{\kappa(y)}.$$ Here $H_{\mathfrak p}$ in the quotient denotes its image after this field extension. Thus every fiber of $\rho$ is affine three-space over its residue field, and the same is true after extension to an algebraic closure. In particular, $\rho$ is surjective. Moreover, $$A=P/(H)\simeq P\otimes_{\Lambda[q]}\Lambda,
 \qquad q\longmapsto0,$$ so $\pi$ is the base change of $\rho$ along the closed immersion $\mathbb A^1_p\hookrightarrow\mathbb A^2_{p,q}$ defined by $q=0$. The fiber assertions and surjectivity follow for $\pi$ as well.

We next check smoothness without assuming local triviality. Let $$\mathfrak j=
 \left(\frac{\partial H}{\partial s},
       \frac{\partial H}{\partial u},
       \frac{\partial H}{\partial F},
       \frac{\partial H}{\partial J}\right)\subset P.$$ For every $\mathfrak p$, differentiation with respect to the coordinate $H_{\mathfrak p}$ in <a href="#fib:residual-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fib:residual-coordinate">[fib:residual-coordinate]</a> gives a $\kappa(\mathfrak p)$-derivation $\delta$ with $\delta(H_{\mathfrak p})=1$. The chain rule expresses $1$ as a linear combination of the four partial derivatives of $H_{\mathfrak p}$. Consequently the image of $\mathfrak j$ generates the unit ideal in $\kappa(\mathfrak p)[\mathbf z]$. If a prime of $P$ contained $\mathfrak j$, localization and reduction over its contraction to $\Lambda$ would give a proper prime of this fiber algebra containing that unit ideal. Hence $\mathfrak j=P$.

As a $\Lambda[q]$-algebra, $P$ has the presentation $$P\simeq \Lambda[q][\mathbf z]/(q-H).$$ The principal open sets on which one of the four displayed partial derivatives is invertible cover $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}P$. On each such open set this presentation has one relation with an invertible Jacobian minor, so it is standard smooth of relative dimension $4-1=3$. Thus $\rho$ is smooth of relative dimension three. Smoothness and relative dimension are preserved by base change, giving the same assertion for $\pi$.

Suppose now that one of the two morphisms is Zariski-locally trivial, and put $$(R_0,B)=(\mathbb C[p],A)
 \quad\hbox{or}\quad
 (R_0,B)=(\mathbb C[p,q],P),$$ respectively, with the algebra structures specified in the statement. In either case, $B$ is a finitely presented $R_0$-algebra, and local triviality implies $B_{\mathfrak m}\simeq R_{0,\mathfrak m}^{[3]}$ for every maximal ideal $\mathfrak m$ of $R_0$. Bass–Connell–Wright’s theorem <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[BassConnellWright](#ref-BassConnellWright), Theorem 3(a)], in the formulation recalled by Gupta <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[GuptaSurvey](#ref-GuptaSurvey), Theorem 4.1], therefore gives an $R_0$-algebra isomorphism $$B\simeq\operatorname{Sym}_{R_0}(M)$$ for a projective $R_0$-module $M$. This conclusion allows arbitrary polynomial transition automorphisms in the assumed local trivializations; no linear structure is assumed beforehand.

The module $M$ is finitely generated. Indeed, if $\mathfrak a$ is the augmentation ideal of $\operatorname{Sym}_{R_0}(M)$, then $M\simeq\mathfrak a/\mathfrak a^2$; finite generation follows because $B$ is a finite-type augmented $R_0$-algebra. Tensoring with each residue field and comparing the dimensions of the resulting polynomial rings shows that $M$ has rank three. For the freeness step, Suslin’s Theorem 2(b)  states that a finitely generated projective module of rank greater than $\dim C$ over $C[t_1,\ldots,t_m]$ is extended from $C$ when $C$ is regular and noetherian. Here $C=\mathbb C$ has dimension zero, giving the needed Quillen–Suslin statement: $M$ is extended from a three-dimensional complex vector space. Thus $M$ is free and $$B\simeq R_0^{[3]}.$$ For $\pi$ this would give $A\simeq\mathbb C[p]^{[3]}\simeq\mathbb C^{[4]}$, contrary to [thm:main](#thm:main). For $\rho$ an $R_0$-algebra isomorphism would give a polynomial coordinate system $P=\mathbb C[p,H,G_1,G_2,G_3]$ for suitable $G_1,G_2,G_3\in P$. In particular, $H$ would be a coordinate of $P$, contrary to Corollary <a href="#cor:stable-coordinate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:stable-coordinate">2</a>. Neither morphism is therefore Zariski-locally trivial. ◻

</details>

The two bases are smooth, normal, and affine over $\mathbb C$. The fiber calculation verifies the residue-field hypothesis in the original affine-fibration assertion of Dolgachev–Weisfeiler, not only its geometric-fiber version. These counterexamples have relative dimension three; they do not address the corresponding assertions in relative dimensions one or two.
